Same shape and same reason as `resize-service.ts` — a page can no more move the OS pointer than resize its own window, and `:hover` is a function of pointer position, not an event, so a synthetic `mouseover` applies no style at all. Split by who can answer: the injected replayer resolves the locator and returns the element's centre `point`; this converts it through `getZoomFactor()` and sends the event. Holds the only genuinely stateful thing here — the last pointer position and whether the button is down — because the OS has exactly one of each. A `press` with no preceding `hover` is refused rather than sent at `(0,0)`; `releaseHeldMouse` is the idempotent safety net called from `withCaptureSuspended`'s `finally`, so a replay that aborts between `press` and `release` can't leave the user's training window in a drag. `resetInputState` clears the point at session start. Covered by `input-service.test.ts`.

- `main/services/resize-service.ts` — applies a `viewport` step during trainer replay by resizing the training window, and reports what it became. `applyViewportStep(win, step, evaluate)` normalizes the size through `normalizeViewport` (the same path the session-open preset takes, so a step and a preset can never resolve to different sizes), calls `setContentSize` (CONTENT, not frame — the recorded number is the page size), reads back `getContentSize()` plus `window.innerWidth/innerHeight` via `PAGE_METRICS_SCRIPT`, and returns the same `{ok,error?,logs}` shape the injected replayer does. `formatResizeLog` prints all THREE sizes because they disagree meaningfully: requested (what the step asked for), window (what the OS granted — it clamps to the display), page (what the document sees — the scrollbar's width is what actually flips a breakpoint). A size the window couldn't reach adds a `warn` line. Measuring is best-effort: a failed readback costs the log its page numbers and nothing else, because the resize itself already happened. The `ResizeHost` interface is deliberately narrow so the logic is testable without an SDK window (`resize-service.test.ts`).
- `main/services/cookie-service.ts` — live cookies + cookie-step application, over `webContents.session.cookies`. Pure helpers `cookieUrlFor` / `specToSetDetails` / `cookieToSpec` are exported for `check:cookie-steps`. **URL reconstruction is the subtle part:** `cookies.set`/`remove` both require a URL, but cookies are stored as Chromium reports them (domain + path). Chromium prefixes a domain cookie with a dot (`.example.com`) which is NOT a hostname — a URL built with it silently targets nothing — and a `Secure` cookie needs an `https` URL since it can't be set over http. `clearCookies` removes each cookie by its OWN reconstructed URL rather than the page URL, so domain cookies don't quietly survive a clear. `applyCookieStep` returns the same `{ok,error?,logs}` shape the injected replayer does, so replay paths treat it uniformly.
- `renderer/main/cookies-panel.tsx` — the Cookies tab (list, add/edit form, per-cookie delete, Clear all, "Add to test as a step").
- `main/services/playwright-runner.ts` — **why a run ended (2026-08-21, R19).** `status` is the exit code and nothing else, so a user Stop and a process timeout both read as "failed". `RunRecord.endedBy` distinguishes them, and `shared/flake-analysis.mjs` filters `"user"` runs out of the verdict beside baseline-update events — a stop between two passes otherwise manufactures two transitions and moves a stable test to "flaky". A `"process-timeout"` is kept: that run really did fail. The reason lives in `endReasons`, a map BESIDE `runs` rather than a field on the handle, because `stop()` deletes the handle at once and `isRunning` is a poll the Stop button reads; it is cleared at the start of every `runCli` so a timed-out browser install cannot attribute its death to the test run that follows it on the same id. `killRun` records the reason before sending the signal, which is a race the two call sites must not each get right by hand. **Who started a run (2026-08-22, R33)** is the neighbouring field and a different question: `start()` takes an optional `trigger` and defaults it to `"manual"` **at that entry point**, so every run this process starts from now on records one and an absent value in history means "predates the field" rather than "a caller forgot". `batch-runner` threads it per RUN rather than stamping the batch record alone — batch history is capped and pruned on its own schedule, so a run outlives the batch that would have explained it. `routine-scheduler.fireRoutine` is the only caller that passes a value, and both routes into it are the schedule: the timer, and the launch prompt accepting an occurrence missed while the app was closed (that prompt asks the user to let an ALREADY-DUE run happen late; it does not make them the person who chose it). **The run outcome also gates run-time Auto-Heal (2026-08-22).** `collectRunHeals(testId, runId, healDir, mode, runPassed)` bakes a healed locator into the test on disk only when `mode === "apply" && runPassed`; a failing apply-run journals the heal as `applied: false` (a suggestion the Heals view offers) and leaves the locator untouched, because a mis-heal usually succeeds at the step and only the run's overall outcome tells you it was right. Exported and driven by `check:heal-journal`'s run-outcome-gate section. See DECISIONS 2026-08-22. **How fast it goes is three layers as of 2026-08-25 (R18)**, resolved by `resolveRunSpeed(params.speed, rec.speed, settings.defaultRunSpeed)` in `shared/run-pacing.mjs`: this run's override, then the test's own pin, then the global default, then `"fast"` as a floor for a caller with no setting to read. It was `rec.speed ?? "fast"`, which is why recordings had to stamp their speed — and why unstamping them needed this first. An unrecognised value at any layer is SKIPPED rather than passed on, since an unknown key reaching `SLOW_MO_MS` resolves to `undefined` and runs the test at full speed. **Handle pop-ups (2026-09-01)** is the same three-layer shape one more time: `resolveHandlePopups(rec.handlePopups, settings.defaultHandlePopups) && !rec.sourceDir` gates `armedPopupRulesFor({ rules, url, handlePopups, disabledPresets })` from `shared/popup-presets.mjs` — the ONE function the trainer and the MCP/CLI runner also call, so the switch cannot be honoured in one process and not another. The run console says which of three things happened, in the run options' own words: `Handling pop-ups: <labels>.` when armed (taught rules by their name, presets by theirs); `Handle pop-ups is off for this test — N overlay rule(s) for this site and the built-in handlers were not armed.` when off with rules it would have used; `Handle pop-ups is off for this test — the built-in pop-up handlers were not armed.` when off with none. A FAILED run whose log says `intercepts pointer events` while the test opted out gets one more line naming the switch — only then, because with handling on the covering element was one nothing matched and pointing at the switch would be wrong.
- `main/services/alert-service.ts` — outgoing webhook alerts. `buildAlertPayload(alert)` is PURE and is the single place the outgoing shape is defined — which is what makes the redaction guarantee enforceable by `check:alerts` (planted secrets never reach the payload; the payload has exactly 5 top-level keys with no passthrough; `detail` keys stay within a known-safe set). **A run alert carries a deep link as of 2026-08-21 (R30):** `RunAlert` requires `testId` and `runId` — required rather than optional, so a caller cannot silently drop the one field that makes the notification actionable — and the payload carries `runId` plus a `link` built by the same `buildDeepLink` the issue-tracker path uses, in `detail` AND in `text` (most webhook consumers render only the latter). The step is deliberately not in the link: `buildDeepLink` takes ids and `failedLabel` is a human string, so a guessed step would open the wrong row. `check:alerts` pins that the link is present, that it is a `goodlooks://` URL and that no http(s) URL rides anywhere in the detail, and round-trips it through `parseDeepLink` so the builder and the parser cannot drift into emitting links that open nothing. Returns null for clean runs / fully-passing batches (a stopped batch alerts even with zero failures, since results are incomplete). `postWebhook(url, payload)` does the fetch (10s `AbortController` timeout, non-2xx → throw) and is exported so the Settings "Send test" button can SURFACE failures; `sendAlert(alert)` is the fire-and-forget path used on the run/batch completion paths and never throws.
- `main/services/webhook-url-store.ts` — the alert URL, encrypted at rest via `safeStorage` (mirrors `anthropic-key-store.ts`: atomic tmp+rename, in-process cache, backend-only). Treated as a SECRET rather than a setting because a Slack/Discord incoming-webhook URL is a bearer credential. `status()` is all the renderer can learn — `{hasUrl, host}`, never the URL. `validateWebhookUrl` accepts http(s) ONLY (a `file://` or `javascript:` URL would otherwise be handed to fetch); `hostOfUrl` gives the display host.
- `main/services/batch-history-store.ts` — persists batches to `userData/recorder/batch-history.json` (capped 50, newest-first). `save()` **upserts by batchId** — the runner calls it once per per-test transition, so appending would leave dozens of records per batch. `reconcileInterrupted()` runs at startup (`main/index.ts`): a record persisted with `running: true` means the app exited mid-batch, so it clears the stale flag, marks the batch stopped, and turns still-running/pending tests into `skipped` with a note — otherwise the UI shows a phantom in-progress batch forever. Idempotent. A corrupt index reads as empty rather than throwing. Deliberately a SEPARATE index from `run-history.json`: a batch is a grouping over runs, and the two prune on different rules.
- `main/services/routine-store.ts` — persists Routines (saved, named jobs) to `userData/recorder/routines.json`. docs/ROUTINES.md capability 1. The file is an **envelope, not a bare array**, for exactly one reason: `migratedFromBatchAt`. `ensureMigrated()` runs once at startup (`main/index.ts`) and is idempotent **by that recorded flag, not by "is the list empty"** — the two differ the moment a user deletes the migrated Routine, and with an emptiness test it comes back on every launch forever. The flag is written even when the migration produced NOTHING, which is the ordinary case. Every read AND write **rebuilds**: a hand-edited file is the same untrusted input an IPC payload is, so engines are filtered through `RUN_BROWSERS` (validate + dedupe + normalise order in one pass), an engineless step is dropped, an unknown failure policy falls back to `continue`, and undeclared keys do not survive. **Two steps naming one test collapse** — the batch runner serialises every entry for one test into a single lane, so keeping both would draw a job that lists four parallel steps and runs three. `normalizeBranchStep` threads the SAME `seen` set through both sides, so a test cannot be on both paths — with a branch that would not merely queue it twice, it would make the two paths overlap and the run would skip an entry it had already executed. A branch with nothing on either side is dropped; one empty side is kept, because "if anything failed run the teardown, otherwise carry on" is exactly that shape. `renameGroup(from, to)` moves every member of a library FOLDER at once, in one write — a renderer loop can land on half the library and the rail would then draw two folders. Case-SENSITIVE, unlike `removeTag`, and the asymmetry is the field's own: a tag is matched (so its spellings must be one chip), a group is only displayed (so two spellings are two folders). `to: ""` is how a group is deleted, because there is no group record to remove. `markTestDeleted()` MARKS rather than removes (ROUTINES open question 4): silently shrinking a saved job is the same class of bug as the batch running fewer tests than it said. `list()` is **oldest first**, not most-recently-edited — a job list that reorders itself on every save moves the row you are looking at. Capped at `MAX_ROUTINES`, counted over the OTHERS so a full index stays editable. Covered by `npm run check:routine-store`.
- `shared/routine-migration.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — the pure conversion from the old Batch settings to a Routine, tested from `main/services/routine-migration.test.ts` (a test beside a `shared/` module matches neither vitest project and passes by never running). Preserves ORDER (`batchOrder`, not `Object.keys`), SELECTION, per-row engines and headedness, and `onFailure: "continue"` — Batch's current unwritten behaviour. **An absent `batchTestOptions` entry is NOT selected**: `defaultRow` in `batch-run-plan.ts` returns `selected: false`, so a test with no stored row is one the user never ticked. Returns `null` rather than an empty Routine, which is also the ordinary outcome for anyone who never opened Batch. Carries the stored concurrency through **without a second ceiling** — `recorderSettingsStore.read()` already clamps it, and a bound declared here could only disagree.
  `MIGRATED_ROUTINE_ID` is fixed rather than random so `ORPHAN_BATCH_OWNER` can name it: a batch with no `routineId` — everything run before Routines shipped, plus the MCP's `run_batch` — belongs to the migrated Routine, because those batches are runs of the old implicit checklist and that Routine IS it. `batchBelongsToRoutine` is the one spelling of that rule, shared because the app filters `Previous batches` with it and an MCP `list_batches` scoped to a Routine will need the same answer.
- `shared/routine-plan.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — what running a Routine queues. **Segments the steps at each `wait`, `notify` and `branch`** and returns `barriers` alongside `perTest`, which is what let the barrier land without a second execution engine; `clampWaitMs` bounds a pause at an hour, because the runner holds the batch open across one. A trailing PAUSE is dropped — computed against the segments that produced ENTRIES, so a wait followed only by deleted tests counts as trailing too. A trailing notify is NOT (the end-of-job message is the one anybody writes) and neither is a trailing branch: it gates nothing, but it still has to mark the losing side skipped or those rows sit at `queued` in the record forever. A branch emits a barrier carrying `{thenSegment, elseSegment}` and queues BOTH sides, advancing the segment counter by three; `hasBranch` then tells a caller that `plannedRuns` is a maximum rather than a count. Flattens groups IN PLACE (a group is structure over the Routine's order, not a second ordering of it) and stamping each member's entry with the group id, which is the whole of what a group means at run time, tested from `main/services/routine-plan.test.ts`. Also exports **`failurePolicy`**, the one normaliser for `RoutineStep.onFailure`: it decides whether the REST of a job runs and it arrives from a file on disk and from the IPC wire, so anything unrecognised — including `skipGroup`, whose groups are capability 3 — becomes `"continue"`. Without it an unknown string would be compared against `"stopRoutine"`, come back false and carry on, which is right today only by accident. A **translation** into the batch runner's existing `{ testIds, perTest }` payload, not a second execution engine: a Routine composes RUNS, so every step becomes its own process, its own `RunRecord` and its own row in Stats. In `shared/` because ROUTINES' rename table says to add an MCP `run_routine` alongside `run_batch` rather than renaming it, so two callers will build this payload. Skips a step whose test is deleted (flagged **or** absent from the library — the flag is what the editor renders, the library is what is true now) and one with no valid engine, and RETURNS the skips rather than swallowing them; `plannedRuns` counts queue entries, not tests, since one step naming two engines makes the two differ. `routineBlockedReason` answers with a sentence and distinguishes an unfinished Routine from one whose tests are all gone — different problems wanting different reactions. Only a Routine that can run NOTHING is refused: a partial skip is a note, or one deleted test would disable a suite.
- `shared/routine-schedule.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — when a Routine is due (ROUTINES capability 2), tested from `main/services/routine-schedule.test.ts`. **An enumeration, not a cron string**, against the spec's sketch: a cron text field's failure mode is a schedule that never fires, which looks exactly like one that is not due yet. Four kinds. `everyMinutes` is anchored to LOCAL MIDNIGHT so the cadence cannot drift, and its step must be in `INTERVAL_STEPS` (5m…24h, all divisors of a day) — a step that is not is refused, not rounded; it **replaced `everyHours`, which migrates on read** (`hours × 60`, exact), because two kinds meaning "on an interval" would be two places to forget. `dailyAt`/`weekdaysAt` are unchanged. `onceAt` holds an absolute INSTANT rather than a wall-clock description, which is what makes "has it fired" a comparison and lets `nextOccurrence` answer it in a line — null afterwards, which already means "never again" to every caller. `nextOccurrence` is STRICTLY after, or a five-minute schedule fires the same occurrence twice, and it builds every answer from local date COMPONENTS rather than adding milliseconds to midnight: a 23-hour day makes "midnight + 1440 minutes" land at 01:00, an hour late on the one schedule whose whole description is "at midnight". `isDue` reads `Routine.lastScheduledRunAt`, which records only SCHEDULED fires: a manual run does not satisfy a schedule. **A one-off is the one exception to "never fired means nothing is owed"** and has its own branch — that rule stops a new cadence running the suite on the spot, but the user picked a DAY, so if the app was shut on it the run is owed; without the branch the catch-up would silently drop it. `withinOnceHorizon`/`onceHorizonEnd` are the three-year cap, and they take `now` — a spent one-off is not invalid, so `normalizeSchedule` stays clockless and only asks whether the instant is a real one. `SCHEDULE_CAVEAT` is the sentence ROUTINES requires the UI to state out loud.
- `main/services/routine-scheduler.ts` — the in-process half of ROUTINES capability 2, started from `main/index.ts`. **Two halves that must not overlap:** the TIMER (60s) fires occurrences arriving while the app is open; the CATCH-UP (`routines:missed`) only REPORTS occurrences missed while it was closed, and the renderer offers them. The split is enforced by `firesNow`'s session bound, not by bookkeeping — without it the catch-up offers a missed run, the user declines, and the timer runs it a minute later. A scheduled run is **always headless** (`forceHeadless`, applied in `routineRunPlan`), overriding the step's own setting. `lastScheduledRunAt` is stamped for EVERY outcome including a collision, or one skipped run becomes a retry every sixty seconds. Declining a missed run stamps it too, or the prompt returns on every launch forever. The tick swallows its own errors (a throw inside an interval stops it silently for the session) and the timer is `unref`'d. Deps are injected so `check:routine-scheduler` drives it with no clock and no browser.
- `main/services/insights/` — **AI insights** (2026-08-18): a scheduled report (daily/weekly/monthly, Settings → Alerts, off by default) written by the configured LLM provider, summarizing the period's runs, trends, site-change signals, expiring Shopify signatures, stalled routine schedules and app release notes, with recommendations that carry validated one-click actions. The app's first UNATTENDED LLM egress, and the payload is summary-shaped by construction: `facts-builder.ts` reads indexes and aggregates (run records, `shared/metrics-query.mjs`, `shared/period-digest.mjs`) and never a log, a script or a header value — pinned at source level plus a planted-secret pass by `check:insights-egress`. `insight-schedule.ts` holds the pure due-rules: calendar-anchored buckets (local day / ISO week / calendar month) decide WHEN, a rolling trailing window decides WHAT, never-generated-and-enabled is due (the toggle is the request), and a failed attempt backs off an hour — stamped at settle, never at start, so a quit mid-generation persists nothing and the period stays due. `insights-service.ts` is the scheduler (60s unref'd tick, deps-injected, started in `whenReady` AFTER `metricsStore.init()` — a tick before the DB opens wouldn't crash, it would confidently report "no failure clusters"): it defers unstamped while `llmService.activeCount() > 0` so a background job never starves an interactive one, refuses to overlap, discards on toggle-off-at-settle, and re-checks nothing it can't. `insight-prompts.ts` interpolates the action kinds from `INSIGHT_ACTION_KINDS` (derived, never transcribed — the llm-knowledge drift rule) and `describeInsightsSending` derives the per-report disclosure from the same payload object the prompt serializes, so the disclosure cannot describe a send the app no longer makes. `parse-insight-response.ts` accepts fenced-JSON (then a widest-span fallback), rebuilds everything field-by-field via the `normalizeInsight*` family in `main/recorder/types.ts` — an action's `testId` must name a test the model was SHOWN — and keeps an unparseable answer as a `degraded` prose report with no actions rather than discarding a paid completion. `release-notes.ts` is the changelog as typed data (bump `package.json` version ⇒ add an entry, same commit); `insights-notifier.ts` posts the local macOS notification (gated on `notifyOnInsightsReady`; failures are never notified — they surface in the view). Renderer: `renderer/main/insights-view.tsx` (route `/insights`, rail row with a cyan unread dot via `RailRow`'s `accessory`, ⌘K entry) renders the numbers strip from builder-computed `stats` (never model prose; null metrics fields are skipped, not zeroed), fixed action verbs per kind (model text cannot relabel a button), and disables an action whose test has since been deleted, rendering the recorded `testName`. The **debug action opens the same AI-debug dialog the Output panel's button opens** — a one-shot intent in `renderer/main/insight-intents.ts` consumed by `test-detail-view.tsx` once its run context exists — so the Sending strip and the manual send survive as the review gate. **Three exits (2026-08-18):** `insights:exportPdf` saves the report as a PDF (`insight-report-html.ts` pure page + `insight-report-pdf.ts` hidden-window print — OUTSIDE `insights/`, because `check:insights-egress` pins that directory filesystem-free and this file writes a user-chosen local file; the report-emitter path+bytes contract, escaping pinned by `insight-report-export.test.ts`); "File issue" files the report as the issue BODY through the standard `DefectSource` pipeline (`kind: "insight-report"`, draft from `insight-report-markdown.ts` — a PDF attachment is not a thing either tracker API accepts); and a new report can announce itself to a dedicated Slack incoming webhook via `alert-service`'s `insightReport` kind (headline + counts only, structurally — see `check:alerts`), gated on `insightsSlackEnabled` with its own encrypted URL (`insights-slack-url-store.ts`, Integrations pane).
- `renderer/main/schedule-picker.tsx` — the schedule chip in the Routine editor (REDESIGN §7.1) and the dialog behind it. The dialog exists to give `SCHEDULE_CAVEAT` room: ROUTINES requires the weaker guarantee to be STATED, and a tooltip does not count when what it qualifies is a promise about unattended work. The chip is NEUTRAL chrome — colour means outcome, and a schedule has not passed or failed anything. Every value comes from a control that cannot produce a bad one (a mode radio, `INTERVAL_STEPS`, native `<input type="time">`, a `Calendar` bounded at the horizon); a time that does not parse is ignored rather than committed as `NaN`. Nothing commits until Save, and each mode keeps its own value while you look at another. "Off" is the only way out of a schedule, so it is a first-class row. **Rebuilt 2026-08-17** (DECISIONS): it was two `Menu`s inside an `AlertDialog`, and `AlertDialogBody` is `overflow-y-auto` while `.gl-menu` is `position: absolute` — so the popover was clipped by its own container and eight of the ten cadences could not be seen. Flat controls are what make that structurally impossible; loosening the body's overflow would only have let the popover escape the panel. The **readout** is the other half of the enumeration argument — it names the next occurrence with `nextOccurrence`, the same function the scheduler calls, so a schedule that never fires stops looking exactly like one that is not due yet — and it is where Save refuses a one-off whose moment has passed, which is the first invalid schedule this dialog has ever been able to produce.
- `renderer/main/missed-runs-dialog.tsx` — the launch prompt, mounted outside `RootShell` beside `LoadFailedDialog` so it can appear on any screen. It is the half that makes `SCHEDULE_CAVEAT` true: the caveat promises a missed run is OFFERED next launch. ONE Routine at a time, oldest first — the runner takes one batch at a time, so offering four would be offering three that get refused. Every answer settles the occurrence, including Escape and the overlay, or the prompt returns on every launch forever.
- `renderer/lib/routine-rows.ts` — the checklist and a Routine, in both directions (REDESIGN §7.1: "the checklist is unchanged; it is now the open Routine's body"). Keeps the Batch view's `order` + `RowOptionsMap` model — its drag handles, engine pills and master headless toggle — and translates that to and from `Routine.steps`, rather than rewriting the checklist against a step array and re-testing every one of those behaviours to gain nothing. **The asymmetry that shapes it:** a checklist row exists for every test in the library, ticked or not; a step exists only for a test that is IN the job. So the ROUTINE is authoritative for what the job contains, and `batchTestOptions` stays as the SCRATCH memory of an unticked row's preferences — untick a row that ran on WebKit and tick it again, and the choice is still there. `rowsFromRoutine` puts the Routine's steps FIRST in step order (a Routine whose four steps are scattered through forty rows is one you cannot see) and forces every other row unticked regardless of what the scratch map says. `stepsFromRows` takes a third map, `PolicyMap` — the per-step `onFailure`. It used to read that off the Routine's previous steps, because the checklist had no control for it and a field the editing surface cannot see is one it must not overwrite; the row gained a control on 2026-08-13, so it round-trips like every other choice and there is one authority instead of two. NOT a field on `BatchRowOptions`, deliberately: that map is the scratch memory of an UNTICKED row, and a policy is a statement about the job — so unticking a row drops its policy where it keeps its engines, or re-ticking would silently re-arm "stop the whole routine if this fails". `sameSteps` compares field by field — and recursively for a group, so a rename or a test moving between groups counts as a change — so opening a Routine cannot re-date it. Groups round-trip through two more maps (`RoutineGroup[]` + `GroupOf`): the checklist stays a FLAT ordered list, which is what lets drag-to-reorder stay what it was, and `stepsFromRows` emits a group at the position of its FIRST member and collects the rest into it, so a member dragged away from its siblings moves within the group rather than tearing it in two. Branches round-trip through two more maps in the same shape (`RoutineBranch[]` + `BranchOf`, which names the side as well), and `stepsFromRows` lets a BRANCH claim a row before a group can — a branch decides whether a step runs at all, where a group only decides what a failure takes out with it. `testCount` counts tests THROUGH groups and through both sides of a branch — `steps.length` stopped being that number the moment one entry could hold many, and the rail said "1 test" for a Routine of two. Round-tripping is pinned by a test.
- `renderer/lib/library-groups.ts` — the library rail's FOLDERS (REDESIGN §7.2), pure. Turns a flat test list into folder rows followed by loose tests: `libraryRows` (folders first and alphabetical, members in library order, collapsed by name, one aggregate dot), `groupNames` (what the "move to…" menu offers) and `toggleCollapsed`. **The set of groups is derived, never stored** — a group is a string on each test (`TestRecord.group`), so this file is the only place that decides what the groups ARE, an emptied folder cannot linger, and there is nothing to garbage-collect. Separate from the rail because every property worth pinning is silent when wrong: a test in two places, a count that disagrees with the rows under it, a whitespace name that draws a titleless folder. **A group is not a tag** — tags are many-to-many labels for SELECTION (the Routine checklist filters by them), a folder is a single PLACE in a navigation rail; see DECISIONS 2026-08-14.
- `renderer/main/group-name-dialog.tsx` — names a folder, for both halves of the same gesture: "New Group…" on a test and "Rename…" on a folder are the same question, and a group is nothing but its name. Refuses an empty name rather than treating it as ungroup — deleting a folder is its own command with its own words, and getting there by clearing a field would be a destructive action indistinguishable from a slip. The field is capped at `MAX_GROUP_LENGTH` so it cannot produce a value the store would truncate.
- `renderer/main/routines-rail.tsx` — the rail's third job (REDESIGN §7.1): on `/batch` it lists saved Routines instead of the library, the way Settings' rail lists panes. SWAPS rather than stacking — two lists in one rail makes the rail a screen of its own, and the checklist's own rows still open a test. The subtitle is a COUNT, not a name list. Marks the FIRST Routine before anything has been chosen, matching what the view opens. `useCreateRoutine` FETCHES AT CLICK TIME rather than subscribing: the hook is called by the rail, which renders on every screen, while its button exists on one. Selection lives in `recorder-store`'s `openRoutineId` — the rail selects it, `batch-view` edits it, and the router cannot carry it (memory history, and a Routine id in an invisible URL).
- `renderer/lib/create-routine.ts` — one definition of "make a new job", shared by the picker and the rail's `+`. Empty rather than a copy of what is on screen; defaults from the globals; `now` and the id suffix passed in rather than read, so a test can pin the id.
- `renderer/main/batch-view.tsx` is now **one Routine's editor** (REDESIGN §7.1). A picker names the saved jobs and carries New/Delete; the name is an ordinary editable field (a plain `<input>`, not the `Input` primitive — its light chrome wins over a theme class by source order and made the name the brightest thing on a screen whose subject is the checklist) committing on blur and Enter, never per keystroke. The checklist is unchanged; it is the open Routine's body, seeded through `rowsFromRoutine`. **Every write goes through one `persist`**: what the job IS to the Routine, what a row REMEMBERS to `batchTestOptions`, with the Routine write skipped when `sameSteps` says nothing changed — `updatedAt` is what the picker sorts by, so writing on a no-op would re-date a job for opening it. Two subtleties, both found by driving it: the re-seed key includes `updatedAt`, or a library refetch racing a save re-seeds from the stale record and an unticked test comes back; and `saveRoutine` bases its patch on **what was last sent while any save is in flight**, or two quick edits (Headless then Capture) each spread their own stale copy and the second silently undoes the first. `captureArtifacts` and `concurrency` are the Routine's `defaults` now, falling back to the global settings only for a user with no Routine; Run goes through `routines:run`, so what executes is the stored record rather than the screen. Capability 3's structure lands in **two menus, not four cells**: one asks what structure a row is part of (top level, a group, or a side of a branch — a row can only be one, so it is one question) and one asks what happens after it (a pause, a message, or both). The checklist overflowed three times before that; when a row runs out of width the answer is to stop asking two questions where there is one. `assignGroup` clears the row's branch and `assignBranch` does NOT clear its group, which is asymmetric on purpose: `stepsFromRows` gives the branch the row and `persist` re-derives `groupOf` from the steps it emitted, so one direction has a backstop and the other has none. A branch header carries the condition as a click-to-flip button; when the `then` side is empty the LONE `Otherwise` header carries it instead, read negated, or the condition would be unreachable. Deleted-test steps are reported above the checklist with a Remove action, since no row can be drawn for a test that is gone. "Reset order" rewrites the STEPS into library order — clearing `batchOrder` alone left the button visible and inert. **The checklist's selection is one tri-state tick (2026-08-25, #74)**, where SELECT ALL and SELECT NONE were two buttons: `selectionState(rowOptions, visibleTests, rowDefaults)` in `batch-run-plan.ts` decides none/some/all and the box both reports and sets it. It answers for the VISIBLE tests — under a tag filter a master computed over the library would read "some" with every row on screen ticked, and clicking it would act on tests the user cannot see. Its accessible name names the SET and does not flip with the state (a checkbox renamed by its own state is announced as a different control on every click), and the count stays in the toolbar, which already reads "N of M selected" over the whole library. **A row's geometry does not depend on its selection (2026-08-25)**: the three cells that only a ticked row has each leave a spacer of the same width AND height, so ticking a box moves no column and changes no row height — `check:batch-row-cells` pins each spacer against its control, and `batch-view.test.tsx` pins that the spacer exists at all. The indeterminate DASH is two rules on `.gl-batch-master-box` rather than a change to `@ui`'s `Checkbox`: Radix renders the Indicator for "some" exactly as for "all", so the shared check mark would draw a partial selection identically to a full one — and adding a dash there would change every checkbox in the app for one caller.
- `renderer/lib/paginate.ts` — pure paging for the Stats lists (`PAGE_SIZE` 50, `DENSE_PAGE_SIZE` 25 for the run history and Step health, `pageCount`, `clampPage`, `pageSlice`, `pageRange`), checked by `check:paginate`.
- `docs/ROUTINES.md` — the design spec **Batch v2 ("Routines") was built from** — its own header records capabilities 1–3 landing 2026-08-13/14 (the Routine entity and migration, the scheduler, and the five step kinds), so read it as rationale rather than a to-do list. Its lasting content is the reasoning: why `batchTestOptions` (a `Record<testId, …>` in settings) cannot express two saved configurations of the same test, why `runFlow` composes STEPS while a Routine composes RUNS, and why the `batch:*` channels and on-disk formats keep their names through any rename.
- **Flows** — the step-level reuse mechanism (`runFlow` composes steps; a Routine composes runs — one composition mechanism, per DECISIONS 2026-08-13). A flow is an ordinary `TestRecord` with `isFlow: true`; `flowParams` names the variables callers may override, and a `runFlow` step carries `flowId` plus per-call `flowArgs` overrides. Generation inlines depth-first with a cycle guard (`expandSteps`/`bindFlowStep` in `script-generator.ts`): every plain variable of the flow binds textually to the flow's own value (caller's argument wins for a declared parameter), while secrets and captured variables stay live `V.x` references and their declarations are merged into the caller's `const V` header — the runner's `variableEnv` walks `runFlow` targets so a flow's secret arrives in the caller's child process env. Every inlined line carries the caller-visible `runFlow` row's `sourceIndex`, so run highlighting points at a row the user can see. **Propagation lives in `testStore.save`:** any save regenerates the spec of every direct and transitive caller (`callersOf`/`regenerateCallers`, spec files only, hand-edited/imported callers skipped) — that is what makes "edit the flow, every caller changes" true on disk. Product surface: the Variables tab's "Reusable flow" section (`variables-panel.tsx`, wired to `tests:setFlow`, which auto-declares a plain variable for any parameter lacking one), per-call overrides in the composer (`step-composer.tsx`) and in `flow-call-editor.tsx` (reached from a `runFlow` row's utilities menu; empty field = follow the default), a `flow` chip on the detail header and a Workflow glyph on the sidebar row. **Library surface:** flows sit in their own "Flows" section at the bottom of the rail (they leave their folder while flagged — the section is their place); the detail view shows a "Used by" strip (`tests:flowUsage`, computed by `callersOf` — there is no stored reverse index) with click-through links, shown for ANY test with callers because the guard below keys on callers, not the flag; `tests:delete` **refuses** while callers exist, naming them (cascading a rewrite of N other tests on a delete would be the bigger surprise); `tests:unwrapFlow` replaces one call with bound copies of the flow's steps — through the exported `flowCallBindings`/`bindFlowStep`, so unwrapping cannot mean something different from running — one level deep, fresh step ids, call-site disabled/continueOnFailure propagated. MCP exposes it read-only: `list_tests` takes an `isFlow` filter and flow rows carry `flowParams`; `get_test` reports both. **Trainer surface:** both trainers multi-select step rows (`renderer/lib/step-selection.ts`; the extraction rule is `shared/flow-extraction.mjs`, one spelling for the button and the backend) and "Create flow" moves a contiguous, block-balanced selection into a new flow via `recorder:extractFlow` (persists immediately; the session stays staged). A `runFlow` row expands to a read-only preview (`flow-steps-preview.tsx`) and from there into **inline editing**: `Session.flowScope` holds a working copy of the flow's steps, the `addStep` funnel and every step method route by id into it while open, the copy travels on the `recorder:flowScope` push (never `recorder:steps`), and `commitFlowScope` writes it back on scope exit — Done, another scope, any whole-list replay, `runner:run`, or finalize; `discardExit` drops it. Whole-list replays expand `runFlow` calls at replay time (`replayEntries`: same bindings as the generator, repeats unrolled, per-flow variable/secret resolution via `runStep`'s scope parameter) and report every inlined step against its call row. Loops: a call carries `repeat` (2–100) or `repeatVar`, emitted as a real clamped `for` — see `repeatSpec`/the loop entries in `script-generator.ts`. Guards: `check:flow-scope` (routing + commit triggers + channel separation), `check:step-ingest` §1b/§1c (loop fields, extraction funnel), assert-parity and step-progress rows for loop semantics and per-iteration reporting.
- `renderer/lib/batch-run-plan.ts` — pure per-row Batch rules (`resolveRow`, `toggleRowBrowser`, `setRow`, `setSelection`, `applyHeadlessToAll`, `pruneRowOptions`, `buildRunPlan`, `resultKeyOf`, `rowStatus`), covered by `batch-run-plan.test.ts`. Exists for the same reason `batch-parallel.ts` does — and to keep ONE copy of the three-deep fallback chain (stored row → the test's own `runBrowser` → the global default), which the row renderer, "select all", the master headless toggle and the run payload all need. Two invariants live here: `toggleRowBrowser` never returns an empty engine list (a ticked test with no engine produces no queue entries and the batch silently runs fewer tests than the toolbar says), and `applyHeadlessToAll` materialises entries for untouched tests (otherwise the master silently skips exactly the rows that look identical on screen to the ones it applied to).
- `renderer/lib/batch-outcome.ts` — what a FINISHED batch adds up to (`batchOutcome`, `batchOutcomeTone`, `batchOutcomeTitle`, `batchOutcomeLabel`), covered by `batch-outcome.test.ts`. Four verdicts — `stopped` / `passed` / `mixed` / `failed` — where `mixed` (some passed AND some failed) is amber and `failed` (something failed, NOTHING passed) is red. **Deliberately a different rule from `rowStatus`**, which stays worst-first: a row is one test, so a webkit failure makes it a broken test regardless of chromium, whereas a batch is a SET of tests and "some passed" vs "nothing passed" is the difference between one bad test and a suite that never started (bad base URL, dead fixture, expired login). Skipped tests are ignored — they reported nothing, so counting them as non-passes would take a clean filtered run amber. `stopped` outranks every verdict for the same reason `running` does in `rowStatus`. `batchOutcomeLabel` stays two words because `StatusChip` is fixed at `--gl-status-w` and CLIPS rather than growing.
- `renderer/lib/batch-order.ts` — pure ordering rules for the Batch view (`applyOrder`, `moveToTarget`, `orderIdsOf`, `orderIsStale`), checked by `check:batch-order`. `moveToTarget` takes IDs rather than indices (filtered views) and recomputes the insert position after removing the dragged id — otherwise dragging downward lands one short of the row you dropped on.
- `renderer/lib/test-tags.ts` — pure tag grouping for the Batch view's filter (`tagCounts`, `untaggedCount`, `hasTag`, `filterByTag`, `parseTagInput`, `ALL_TAGS`/`UNTAGGED` sentinels). Matches case-insensitively so a filter survives a tag being re-cased on another test. Does NOT normalize — that's `normalizeTags`'s job backend-side.
- `shared/user-data-rules.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — WHERE THE APP'S DATA LIVES, as rules. Two processes resolve it independently (`main/shell/user-data.ts` and `mcp/data-dir.mjs`) and **both write** — the MCP appends run history and batch history — so a disagreement is the app reading a library the server is not writing to, which is the bug `user-data.ts` was itself written against, one process over. The probing cannot be shared (`shared/` is pure by rule, and one side is compiled TypeScript while the other is plain `.mjs` with no build step), so what is shared is the part that drifts silently: `USERDATA_OVERRIDE_ENV`, `STORE_SUBDIR`, `STORE_MARKERS`, `LEGACY_DIR_RE` and `chooseDataDir` — override, then own-store, then adopt the newest legacy store in place, then the default. `STORE_MARKERS` is scoped under `recorder/` because Electron writes Cache/ and friends into userData before the app's own code runs, and a top-level marker would report every fresh profile as an established store. `check:mcp-parity` §13 drives both resolvers against one fixture tree and asserts they agree, including the ordering and which branch fired.
- `mcp/data-dir.mjs` (+ `.d.mts`) — the MCP's half of the above, replacing `glaze-data.mjs`. **That file is why the server could not start for six months**: it resolved from `package.json`'s `id`, a Glaze identity the SDK port removed, so it threw at module load and every one of the 23 tools was unreachable. `readProjectId` and `readHostFlavor` are gone with it — both described a layout that no longer exists. Derives Electron's default from **`productName`**, which Electron PREFERS over `name` (so `Good Looks!`, not `good-looks` — reading the wrong one lands beside the real store, which is the same misdirected-read bug). Handles win32/linux as well as darwin, not because the app ships there but because that is what lets `check:mcp-boot` run in CI. `appName(projectRoot)` takes its root as a parameter because `import.meta.url` moves when a caller bundles the file, which `check:mcp-parity` does. **`appVersion(projectRoot)`** is its sibling, added 2026-09-03 for the same reason a version should be read rather than stated: `server.mjs` announced `version: "1.0.0"` to every client in the initialize handshake, a literal that was correct on the day it was typed and stale from the next bump. Nothing could have noticed — no tool returns it, and a wrong version in a handshake is not an error, just an answer. `name` deliberately stays `package.json`'s `name`: it is the identifier clients key their configuration off, not a display name.
- `main/services/__tests__/runtime-boot.check.ts` (`check:runtime-boot`) — **boots `glaze-runtime.mjs` through the real Playwright CLI**, which is the only place its Babel transform exists. Writes the real emitted runtime and the real emitted config into a temp scripts dir, then feeds the CLI one REAL `generateSpec` output per helper plus a spec importing every export, and asserts all of them are collected and that the exports arrive as functions. It exists because nothing did: the unit tests and `e2e/assert-parity.spec.ts` load the emitted module through Node's own loader, where `import.meta` is legal, and `e2e/step-progress.spec.ts` writes the file but generates only plain click/assert specs that never import it — so all of them stayed green while every helper-using test in the app failed at collection. The helper list is read off the emitted source by regex rather than typed in the check, and a coverage assertion fails when an export has no row, so a helper added later cannot quietly go unbooted. No browser is launched (collection plus one fixture-free test), so it costs about a second and runs anywhere `npm install` ran. See DECISIONS 2026-08-21.
- `mcp/boot.check.mjs` (`check:mcp-boot`) — **spawns the server and speaks stdio JSON-RPC to it**: initialize, then `tools/list`. The only check that asks whether a client gets a usable server, and the reason it exists is that nothing did — `check:mcp-parity` and `check:mcp-select` read the source and import the pure modules, and were green for the entire six months the server was dead. Since 2026-09-03 it also pins WHICH BUILD the handshake names — `serverInfo.version` against `package.json`'s `version`, over the wire rather than against `appVersion()`, because the bug was never in reading `package.json` but in the server not asking. Also pins the failure path: no store means exit 1 with a sentence naming the override, not a stack trace from inside `node_modules`. Drives everything through `GOOD_LOOKS_USERDATA` at a throwaway store, which is what makes it platform-independent.
- `mcp/run-pool.mjs` — pure bounded-concurrency pool for the MCP `run_batch` tool (`runPool`, `clampParallel`, `MAX_PARALLEL`). Claims items in order, so `parallel: 1` is exactly a sequential loop; a throwing worker never aborts the pool. **No lanes here, unlike the app** — `executeTest` mints a fresh uuid per run and never keys by `testId`, so repeat entries are safe once each run has its own `PW_OUTPUT_DIR`. Pinned against the app's clamp in `check:batch-runner`.
- `mcp/playwright-config.mjs` + `main/services/playwright-config-source.ts` — the generated `playwright.config.ts`, held as a string in two places because the MCP server is standalone `.mjs` and cannot import app code. **Both processes write the same path in the shared scripts dir**, so whichever ran last wins; `check:runner-config` pins them byte-identical and asserts each env-driven field (`PW_OUTPUT_DIR`, `PW_TEST_TIMEOUT_MS`, `PW_SLOWMO_MS`) is still there. `outputDir` is the live reason: parallel runs each need their own Playwright scratch directory, since Playwright derives the default from the SPEC's path and two runs of one spec would share (and clean) one folder.
- `mcp/select-tests.mjs` — pure selection for the MCP `run_batch` tool (`selectTests`, `summarizeResults`). Mirrors the app's semantics: explicit ids run in the order given and are honored even for hidden tests; tag/all selections exclude hidden tests; a requested id that doesn't exist is returned in `missing` rather than silently dropped.
- **`shared/` — the one pure core both the app and the MCP server import** (added 2026-08-07). Plain ESM with a hand-written `.d.mts` beside each file, following `mcp/select-tests.mjs`'s precedent: the app is TypeScript compiled by Vite/tsc against `@glaze/core` and the MCP is standalone `.mjs` with no build step, so they cannot share a `.ts` module — but they *can* share this, and `npm run type-check` still checks every TypeScript caller through the declaration. **Admission rule: pure only** — no `fs`, no `@glaze/core`, no IPC, no `process`. Anything needing the filesystem stays on its own side and hands data in.
  - `shared/run-pacing.mjs` — `SLOW_MO_MS`, `slowMoFor`, the per-test timeout bounds (`MIN`/`MAX`/`DEFAULT_TEST_TIMEOUT_MS`, `clampTestTimeoutMs`, `isTestTimeoutMs`), `CRAWL_MIN_TEST_TIMEOUT_MS` and `resolveTestTimeoutMs`. Was `main/services/run-pacing.ts` plus three transcriptions of the delay table; `run-pacing.ts` and `recorder-settings-store.ts` now re-export from here, and `renderer/lib/llm-prompts.ts` imports `slowMoFor` instead of keeping its own copy.
  - `shared/proxy-config.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — the proxy settings' rules: the traffic/source vocabulary (`ProxyTraffic` = none/app/test/both, `ProxySource` = automatic/manual) and guards, `PROXY_DEFAULTS`, `normalizeProxyUrl` (scheme allowlist http/https/socks5, canonical `scheme://host:port`, and a hard REFUSAL of credentials in the URL — the URL is stored in plain settings JSON, the password is encrypted, and accepting `user:pass@host` would re-create the plaintext copy that split exists to prevent), `proxySettingsFrom` (rebuild-don't-filter over the raw file), `proxyAppliesTo`/`manualProxyFor`, `playwrightProxyEnv` (the ONE rule for when a run gets `PW_PROXY_*` — the password is a parameter, so the MCP passes null and says so) and `isLoopbackHost`. Shared for the user-data-rules reason: the app and the MCP server both act on the same settings file, and the direction a transcribed copy fails is an MCP run taking a different network path from the identical app run, which no output would mention. `check:mcp-parity` §3b pins the agreement.
  - `shared/cost-units.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — what the Cost panel's money is denominated in and what a CI minute is published to cost. `COST_CURRENCIES` (six majors plus `none`, which restores the bare numbers the panel shipped with), `CI_RUNNER_PRESETS` (GitHub's five standard hosted-runner rates, copied by hand on 2026-08-12 and not tracked), `runnerForRate`/`rateForRunner`, and the two clamps + defaults (`clampCostPerCiMinute`, `clampMinutesPerManualRun`, `COST_DEFAULT_PER_CI_MINUTE`, `COST_DEFAULT_MINUTES_PER_MANUAL_RUN`). In `shared/` because three places need the same lists and one of them is the **settings store's validation** — a currency id off disk is untrusted the way anything read off disk is, and a transcribed copy of the table would be right the day it was written. `runnerForRate` compares with a 1e-9 tolerance, not `===`: the price field steps by 0.001 and stepping up lands on `0.010000000000000002`, which a strict compare reports as "Custom" over a price the control itself produced.
  - `shared/batch-queue.mjs` — `buildQueue`, the dataset-sweep expansion **and** the per-row engine fan-out. Also copies each step's `onFailure` onto every entry it fans out to, so the runner asks the thing that failed rather than re-deriving which step it belonged to — one red engine of a three-engine step is enough to trigger its policy. Was in `batch-runner.ts`, which re-exports it; the MCP's `run_batch` calls the same function, so a sweep queues identically on both sides, and `run_routine` passes `routineRunPlan`'s `perTest` through it rather than looping over the engines itself. The nesting order is the load-bearing part: both expansions are ENGINE-MAJOR *inside* a test and never across tests, because the runner groups the queue into lanes by `testId` and a test's entries must stay contiguous for that grouping to reproduce queue order.
  - `shared/strip-ansi.mjs` — `stripAnsi`. Was in `playwright-runner.ts`, which re-exports it (so `strip-ansi.test.ts` is unchanged).
  - `shared/playwright-config-source.mjs` — `playwrightConfigSource` + `PLAYWRIGHT_CONFIG_FILE` + `DEFAULT_EXPECT_TIMEOUT_MS`. Shared because both processes write **the same file** in the same scripts dir, so two spellings meant the last writer won: the MCP's copy carried no `timeout` line at all. Declares `expect.timeout` (10s, matching the app's own `DEFAULT_WAIT_TIMEOUT_MS` rather than Playwright's built-in 5s — an explicit "wait until" step got 10s while the assertion after a navigating click, which IS the navigation wait, got half that) and `trace: "retain-on-failure"`, so a failed run leaves the one artifact that answers "what did the page actually look like".
  - `shared/run-comparison.mjs` — `compareReplays`, the then-vs-now step verdicts. `main/services/run-comparison.ts` keeps `compareRuns`, which does the two artifact reads and delegates.
  - `shared/error-signature.mjs` — `errorSignature` (normalize a failure into a clustering key) + `firstErrorLine` (find the failure in a run log). Moved out of `flake-analysis.ts`, which re-exports it, because `runs.error_signature` is computed at metrics INGEST — the log it comes from is capped at 1000 runs and pruned out from under any later reader. `firstErrorLine` skips Playwright's `Error Context:` trace pointer, which otherwise matched first for 109 of 207 failing runs and made "a file path" the most common failure in the whole history.
  - `shared/metrics-schema.mjs` — DDL, `PRAGMAS`, column orders and the positional `bind`. `SCHEMA_VERSION` and **no incremental migrations**: a version mismatch drops and replays, because the source of truth is still on disk and "migrate" and "rebuild" produce identical results with only one of them able to be got subtly wrong.
  - `shared/rollup.mjs` — `rollupRun`, run + artifacts → rows. Pure and **idempotent by construction**, which is what lets run-completion and the prune preflight both reach the same run. Owns the translation between the app's two step-index spaces (see `ReplayStep.actionIndex`).
  - `shared/metrics-query.mjs` — the curated queries: `stepHealth`, `stepDurationTrend`, `suiteCost`, `browserMatrix`, `failureClusters`, `runEvidence`, `siblingRuns`, `stepDurations` (p50/p95 over two windows), `stepBrowserMatrix` (per STEP, where `browserMatrix` is per test — "step 7 fails on WebKit" sends you to one locator, "this test fails on WebKit" sends you to read the whole test), plus `counts`/`isPopulated`. **`siblingRuns(db, testId, { stepId })` (2026-08-22)** joins in `step_status` — the asked-for step's own outcome in each sibling, null when that run never executed it — as a subquery rather than a JOIN (a looped step has one row per iteration; a failed one wins), so triage can tell "passed on webkit" from "never ran there". `stepDurations` computes its percentiles in JS rather than SQL, deliberately: the `LIMIT 1 OFFSET <expression over aggregates>` form SQLite accepted for p95 and rejected for p50 with "datatype mismatch", and because `all()` swallows a throw by contract the broken half came back as `null` and read exactly like "this step was never timed". **No SQL escapes this file** — not into a tool body, a React component or an emitter. That is what makes the plan's "strictly local, per-machine" decision reversible, and why the MCP exposes curated tools instead of a SQL escape hatch. Every function tolerates a null handle, because metrics are unavailable on a runtime without `node:sqlite` and "no data" has to be an ordinary answer.
  - `shared/a11y-rollup.mjs` — `rollupA11y`, `selectLatestA11yRuns`, and the violation identity pair `violationKey` / `keysOf`. The identity pair was previously TWO hand-copies — `main/services/a11y-diff.ts` and `renderer/lib/a11y-format.ts`, each carrying a "keep in sync" comment — and both now re-export from here. That drift fails silently in the worst direction: two spellings of a key mean an accepted violation stops matching its baseline, so the app reports a finding the user has already dismissed. `selectLatestA11yRuns` is shared for the same reason across a process boundary — the Stats tile counts from the run list in a query cache while the `a11y:rollup` handler reads replay files off disk, and only a shared rule keeps them describing the same runs. `rollupA11y`'s impact counts OVERLAP deliberately (a step with a critical and a moderate violation is in both), because filing each step under its worst severity alone hides every moderate rule that shares a step with a critical one. Covered by `main/services/a11y-rollup.test.ts`.
  - `shared/flake-analysis.mjs` — `analyseFlake`, the stability verdicts (`flaky` / `changed-since` / `data-dependent` / …), `countTransitions` and `MIN_RUNS_FOR_VERDICT`. Moved here from `main/services/flake-analysis.ts` in Phase 4 so `get_flake_report` serves the same verdicts the app's Stability panel shows — an MCP that re-derived "flaky" from its own rules would disagree with the screen the user is looking at. `main/services/flake-analysis.ts` re-exports it; `main/services/flake-source.ts` keeps the I/O half (logs, replays, the heal journal), which is what let the analysis move at all. The move also retired a real copy: `renderer/lib/recorder-types.ts` had a hand-written mirror of the verdict union and the threshold, because a renderer cannot import from `main/` — it can import from here, so the mirror is gone and `check:flake-analysis` now pins that no copy is REINTRODUCED rather than that two copies agree. **Transitions are segmented by ENGINE as of 2026-08-25 (R20)**, and the flake rate divides by the adjacent same-engine pairs they were counted over. Counting the interleaved sequence made a test that passes on Chromium and Firefox and fails every time on WebKit read as four state changes — so the cross-browser matrix degraded the stability signal the more it was used. `analyseBrowsers` mirrors `analyseDatasets`, guards included (two engines minimum, no un-attributed runs), and earns the same kind of verdict: `browser-dependent`, ranked beside `data-dependent` because both name a cause. `FlakeRun.runBrowser` needed no migration — both callers already pass whole `RunRecord`s.
  - `shared/step-insights.mjs` — what the step rows MEAN, separately from how they are fetched: `divergentSteps` (a step × engine matrix reduced to one verdict per step), `slowdowns` (steps whose median grew past `SLOWDOWN_RATIO`, guarded by `MIN_SAMPLES_FOR_TREND` in BOTH windows) and `costBreakdown`. Kept out of `metrics-query.mjs` because that file has one job and one rule, and out of both callers because the app's panel and the MCP's tool must not be able to disagree about what "diverges" means. `divergentSteps`'s vocabulary deliberately matches `triage.mjs`'s cross-run signals — the same question asked of a whole history rather than one run. **`insufficient` is not `clean`**: 255 of 305 steps on the development machine have only ever run on one engine, and rendering those as clean would give a single-engine suite a clean bill of cross-browser health.
  - `shared/triage.mjs` — `triageRun`, site problem or runner problem, plus the `TRIAGE_COHORT` sibling window both callers must share (or the same run answers differently from the app and from the MCP). Takes a bundle somebody else has read — `runEvidence()`'s run + steps, `siblingRuns()`, and the failing step's `stepHealth()` row — and returns `{ verdict, confidence, evidence, limits, suggestedNext }`. **Evidence is the product; the verdict is a summary of it.** Four rules it is built to: `unknown` is first-class (a run with no artifacts must say so, not reason from pass/fail); it never throws and never touches a run's status, pinned at source level by `check:triage`; anything argued from an ABSENCE is withdrawn when `console_dropped`/`network_dropped` say the absence isn't real; and `limits` costs confidence per entry, so three blind spots are not priced as one. Weighted signals, not booleans — a 5xx on the failing step and a changed screenshot are both site-ward and are not the same claim. **The cross-run signals are scoped to the failing STEP (2026-08-22):** a sibling counts only if its `step_status` says it executed the step, its outcome is the step's rather than the run's, and the siblings set aside are a `limits` entry — because read off whole runs, a test's older shape "passed on webkit" for a step it never had, and a strict-mode violation was filed as an engine problem. `ambiguous-locator` is STRONG for the same failure: Playwright naming the cause must outrank a cross-run inference.
  - `shared/failure-reasons.mjs` — the failure-reason vocabulary: `DEFAULT_FAILURE_REASONS` (six built-ins, adapted from mabl's defaults minus the two this app cannot have — a11y findings never fail a run, and there are no performance tests), `resolveFailureReason(id, custom)` (id → current definition; a DISABLED custom reason still resolves so history keeps its name), and `suggestFailureReason(triage, errorLine)` — the deterministic mapping from a triage result's strongest signal (or a connect-level error line, checked first because a failed connection prevents every other kind of evidence) to a built-in reason id. Shared because three consumers must agree: the runner assigns automatically at run end, the run panel and Stats resolve names for display, and the MCP resolves the same ids and suggests from the same mapping in `triage_run`. **Deliberately not an LLM**: the one unattended LLM egress this app allows (insights) is fenced away from logs and scripts, which is exactly the evidence a categorizer needs — so automatic assignment is the triage mapping, custom reasons are manual-only, and mabl's "agent guidelines" field has no counterpart here. Suggests nothing rather than "other" when no signal fires: an uncategorized failure invites a human answer, a guessed one looks answered. Field limits for custom reasons (`MAX_REASON_NAME` 100, `MAX_REASON_DESCRIPTION` 200, `MAX_ACTIVE_CUSTOM_REASONS` 50) live here so the store and the Settings editor cannot drift. Covered by `failure-reasons.test.ts`.
  - `shared/run-trigger.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — **who started a run (2026-08-22, R33).** `RUN_TRIGGERS` (`manual` / `schedule` / `mcp` / `cli`), `normalizeRunTrigger`, and `RUN_TRIGGER_DESCRIPTIONS` — the sentence each trigger MEANS, which is the only naming table because the Stats row marks a trigger with a glyph rather than a word, so the sentence is what the icon carries for hover and assistive tech. In `shared/` because **there are already two writers in two processes**: the app's `run-history-store.append` and `saveRunRecord` in `mcp/store.mjs`, which writes the same `run-history.json` without importing a line of the app. The CLI is the third, and adding its value here rather than in three places is what this file exists for. **It landed as `cli`, not the `ci` this entry used to promise (2026-08-26, R6)**, and the reason is the axis: the same command is what the documentation tells a developer to run on their laptop, so recording that as CI would invent evidence for the one filter the value serves. Read the four back and they are all the same kind of answer — WHICH ENTRY POINT started this. "Was it CI?" is `RunRecord.provenance` below, which answers it from the environment instead of inferring it. **The value was a literal `"mcp"` inside `mcp/run-tests.mjs` until R6**, which was right while the MCP server was its only caller and became silently wrong the day `cli/run.mjs` imported the same runner: every `good-looks run`, this repo's own GitHub Action included, was filed as "Started by an MCP client". It is a parameter with **no default** now — a default of `"mcp"` is the reflex repair and rebuilds the same bug for the next caller. **One axis only — who initiated the run, never what it executed.** `replay` is deliberately not a member: a re-run is `RunRecord.replayOfRunId`, which names the run it replays and stays true whoever pressed the button; folding it in here would make a replay started by a person and one started by an agent the same value, which is the one question the field exists to answer. `normalizeRunTrigger` returns `undefined` for anything unrecognised, and **absent is UNKNOWN, never `manual`** — the scheduler and the MCP server were both writing runs to this store months before the field existed, so defaulting on read would invent evidence (the `speed` rule, not the `runBrowser` one). **The renderer narrows again on READ** (`TriggerMark` in `stats-view.tsx`): `run-history-store` narrows in `append`, but `readAll` casts the parsed JSON straight to `RunRecord[]`, so the write-side guard does nothing for a value already on disk — and an unknown trigger used as a key into the glyph map took the whole Stats view down to a blank screen. Pinned by `main/services/run-trigger.test.ts` and by two blocks in `check:mcp-parity` (§14), which run what each writer's source actually writes through the app's own normalizer rather than matching a string.
  - `shared/run-provenance.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — **where a run came from (2026-08-26, R6).** `readRunProvenance(env)` reads the commit, branch, repository and job out of an environment; `normalizeRunProvenance` is the gate everything passes through. `trigger` above says which entry point started a run; this says **what was under test**, which on a runner is the only question that makes a run history worth keeping — without it, forty container runs a week are forty rows nothing can tell apart, and a suite that reddened on one commit and passed on the next reads as flake. **Not backfillable**, like `trigger`: the environment that would have answered it is gone with the container, which is the argument for adding it ahead of the surfaces that read it and the reason **R12's ingest is blocked on it** — ingesting foreign runs before the library can say they are foreign writes rows nothing can ever classify. Two sources per field, explicit beating derived (`GOOD_LOOKS_REVISION` / `_BRANCH` / `_REPOSITORY_URL` / `_JOB_URL`, then GitHub Actions); any other CI uses the four generic names, which is the documented contract rather than a gap. **`GITHUB_HEAD_REF` is preferred over `GITHUB_REF_NAME`** because the latter is `42/merge` on a pull request, naming no branch anyone can check out — and chosen by whoever opened the PR, which is what makes the gate load-bearing. **Every field is untrusted text**, so each is refused independently for length, for a control character, or (the two URLs) for any scheme but http/https via `normalizeBaseUrl`. The rule that is not the obvious one: **reject, never truncate** — a truncated sha is a shorter value of the right shape, naming a different commit or none, and everything downstream will compare it against real ones; absent says "unknown", which is true. Refusing one field never refuses the others, because a hostile branch name must not cost the run its revision. `run-history-store.append` narrows again on its own write, since three processes write that file. Pinned by `main/services/run-provenance.test.ts`, by `check:mcp-parity` §18, and end-to-end through the spawned binary in `check:cli-exit`.
  - `shared/run-ingest.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — **the gate a foreign run record crosses (2026-08-26, R12).** `normalizeIngestedRun` narrows one record from another machine; `planIngest` splits a batch into fresh, already-present and unusable. Needed because a container's library dies with the container: without ingest, a suite running forty times a week in CI leaves no trace in the app, and Stability, the flake verdict and step health keep answering from the handful of runs someone triggered by hand. **Two fields are dangerous and both are a stored path used without a containment check.** `logFile` is absolute and written by the other machine; `runHistoryStore.readLog` reads it with no check, and `searchLogs` reads every live record's and returns an excerpt around each match — so a stored foreign path is readable through the app's search box. The gate therefore **returns no `logFile` at all**, and the caller derives one from the id, which is the same rule `shared/script-path.mjs` applies to `scriptPath`. `id` becomes a filename (`<logs>/<id>.log`), so it is validated as a safe token — first character alphanumeric, no separators, which is what rejects `.` and `..` without a special case. **Rebuilds rather than filters**, so an unknown key cannot ride in. Required fields missing ⇒ the record is refused whole (there is no honest value to invent for a status or a start time); optional fields are judged **independently**, so one bad field does not lose a run whose outcome and timing are real. `trigger` and `provenance` go through their own gates rather than a second copy. `hasTrace` is dropped because the trace does not travel; `failedStepIndex`/`stepCount` are dropped because `mcp/run-tests.mjs` writes them, `RunRecord` declares neither, and nothing app-side reads them off a run. Pinned by `main/services/cli-ingest.test.ts` and `check:run-ingest`, which also asserts the gate and `RunRecord` still agree in both directions.
  - `cli/export.mjs` + `shared/export-bundle.mjs` (+ `.d.mts`) — **`good-looks export --out DIR` (2026-08-26, R10).** Writes a library a build server can run: `recorder/tests.json` and `recorder/scripts/`, and nothing else. It replaces copying the app's data directory by hand, which was a leak waiting for somebody in a hurry — that directory also holds run history, every log and screenshot, `metrics.db`, saved login sessions, an LLM API key, the alerts webhook URL and the encrypted secrets store. **The rules are an allowlist in both directions, and both halves matter.** The FILES are named positively, because a denylist is right the day it is written and silently wrong the day a new store file lands beside the others — and the new file is the one nobody has thought about. The FIELDS are too: an exported record is REBUILT from named keys rather than spread and pruned, the same rule `normalizeRawStep` applies at the capture boundary and for the same reason. The field rule is **"a field is exported because a run reads it"** — a field no runner reads is inert in the bundle, which is R49's shape — and `check:export-egress` reads `TestRecord` out of `main/recorder/types.ts` and fails on any key in NEITHER list, so a new field cannot ship because nobody looked. **No path from the authoring machine travels**: `scriptPath` becomes the position inside the bundle and `sourceDir` becomes `imported/<id>`, which is truthy and pathless — dropped, it would flip an imported test from uninstrumented to instrumented on the runner, since the unattended runner reads that field as a boolean. **Secret VALUES cannot travel** (they are encrypted to the app), so a test declaring one is exported and NAMED with the variables it wants: refusing the whole export over two tests out of thirty would be refusing the feature, and letting a CI operator find out at a login form is the failure that names instead. A directory rather than an archive — a bundle is a thing somebody reviews before committing. Copying an imported test takes its whole sandbox so its relative imports resolve, and a symlink is skipped rather than followed, the same rule `copyRelativeImports` applies on the way in. Tests: `main/services/export-bundle.test.ts` and `main/services/cli-export.test.ts`, the latter building a store full of credentials and asserting on what did NOT come out.
  - `shared/heal-evidence.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — `normalizeHealPageUrl` / `normalizeHealRect`, the two narrowings a heal's page evidence goes through. Written for `main/recorder/types.ts` in PR 1 of the preemptive-updates plan and MOVED here (2026-09-01) when a second process needed the identical rules: `good-looks ingest` carries heals back from CI runners through a gate written in plain `.mjs`, and the URL narrowing is a PRIVACY rule — sensitive query values elided by name, http(s) only, over-long rejected rather than truncated — that must not depend on which machine healed. `types.ts` re-exports both, so every existing caller is unchanged and there is still one spelling. The control-character test is a scan rather than a regex literal, because `main/services/frame-escape.test.ts` exists to catch exactly the class of edit that silently mangles one (and did, mid-slice, which is how the guard proved itself).
  - `shared/heal-ingest.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — **the gate a foreign HEAL crosses (2026-09-01, the plan's Later item 2).** Same shape as `run-ingest.mjs` and one thing more dangerous: a run record is read, but a heal IS A LOCATOR, one accepted click from being generated source Playwright executes in Node (`ingested heal → donor → proposal → accept → step.locator → generateSpec`). So `normalizeIngestedHeal` rebuilds from named keys, `normalizeIngestedLocator` rebuilds each locator value by value within depth and width bounds (nothing survives that the walk did not copy — no functions, no prototype keys), and the page URL goes through `heal-evidence.mjs` itself rather than a copy. **Four fields are the ingesting machine's, not the container's**: the `id` is minted locally (a foreign id collides with a local entry's, and every accept/revert is keyed by it), `testId`/`runId` come from the ENVELOPE the artifact was found in (an event naming its own test could attach a heal to a test it never touched), and `applied` is always false — nothing here changed, so `applied: true` would offer a revert that writes a locator the user's test never had. It costs the donor nothing, because `donorsFromJournal` accepts a run heal whose RUN passed and that run record is ingested alongside. `candidates` are deliberately dropped: the Heals view offers them as locators to write into a test, and a foreign menu is a wider door than the fix itself for no gain the engine can use. `planHealIngest` mirrors `planIngest`, keyed `runId::stepId` — one heal per step per run, so a second ingest adds nothing while the same step healing on two runs stays two events, which is the decay signal `list_heals` reports.
  - `cli/ingest.mjs` — **`good-looks ingest DIR` (2026-08-26, R12).** The disk half: locates the library (accepts the artifact unpacked at either level, and says which it used), reads the history, plans, copies each log by id, stamps `ingestedAt`, and writes through `saveRunRecords`. Deduped by id, so running it twice is safe and the second run ingests nothing — which matters because the natural way to use it is to point it at whatever artifact directory is on hand. Refuses this library's own directory by name rather than reporting every record a duplicate. `--dry-run` and `--json`. **Carries the runs' HEALS too, as of 2026-09-01**: after the records are stored it reads each fresh run's `run-heals.json` artifact (path derived from validated ids, never from anything the record carried — the `logFile` rule), gates the events through `shared/heal-ingest.mjs`, mints an id per entry and merges them into `recorder/heal-journal.json`, capped per test the way the app's own store caps it. Scoped to runs it actually stored, because a heal naming a run this library does not have points at nothing; skipped entirely on `--dry-run`; and it never truncates a journal it failed to parse, which would trade the user's whole heal history for one CI import. Its tests live in `main/services/cli-ingest.test.ts`, because a `.test.ts` under `cli/` matches neither Vitest project.

  - `shared/run-history-rules.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — **how run history is CAPPED, and what is remembered when the cap drops a run (2026-08-22).** `RUN_HISTORY_CAP`, `PRUNED_DAYS_KEPT`, `dayStartOf`, `emptyPrunedTally`, `readPrunedTally` (the all-or-none read) and `foldPrunedRun`. Both processes append to `recorder/run-history.json`, so both decide when it is full — and they had drifted for months. PR #158 raised the app's cap from 1000 to 50000 and built the pruned tally underneath it across **37 files, none of them under `mcp/`**, so the server kept the pre-#158 policy: cap 1000, no tally. One MCP run against a store the app had grown past a thousand records truncated it to a thousand, deleted the dropped runs' logs, and took those runs out of the lifetime totals permanently — measured at **49,001 records pruned by a single append**. Nothing throws; the Stats board just reports a smaller history than the machine has, which is the exact bug #158 existed to end. The fs stays on each side (one has a logger and an Electron userData path, the other has neither); what is shared is the rule. Pinned by `main/services/run-history-rules.test.ts` and by `check:mcp-run-history`, which drives BOTH writers against one 50,000-record fixture and compares outcomes rather than restating either constant.
- `mcp/run-history.mjs` (+ hand-written `.d.mts`) — appending a run to the app's history from the standalone server, kept out of `server.mjs` for the same reason `run-plan.mjs` is: importing `server.mjs` starts an MCP server on stdio, so anything defined there can only be tested by reading its source. **That is how the cap drift survived** — every `check:mcp-*` reads source or imports pure modules, and none of them could append a run and look at the file afterwards. `dataDir` is a parameter rather than a module-level constant, which is the whole point: it makes the function drivable against a fixture tree. Prunes to `RUN_HISTORY_CAP` and folds every dropped run into the tally, both from `shared/run-history-rules.mjs`.
- `mcp/run-plan.mjs` — what an MCP run is *made of*, kept out of `server.mjs` so `check:mcp-parity` can exercise it (importing `server.mjs` starts an MCP server on stdio). `runEnv` / `runArgs` build the child process's environment and CLI arguments; `sanitizeOutput` is the single choke point everything written or returned passes through; `secretVariableNames` and `datasetRow` read a test's variables; `consoleNetworkWithheldReason` decides whether a run's console/network may be served; `describeRun` reports what a run did NOT do.
- **`bin/good-looks.mjs` + `cli/` — the `good-looks` CLI (2026-08-25, R3 + R2).** The THIRD CALLER of the run path, which is the load-bearing decision of `docs/plans/test-runner-improvements.md` §3.3 and the reason the extraction (`mcp/run-tests.mjs`) came first: `runSelection` returns a result, so a CLI can render it without being an MCP tool. There is no runner here — `cli/run.mjs` resolves a data directory, calls `runSelection`, and turns the result into text and a number.
  - `bin/good-looks.mjs` is deliberately thin, because its job is the one thing a test cannot drive: touching `process`. It sets **`process.exitCode` and returns**, never `process.exit(n)` — that terminates before a pending stdout write flushes, and stdout to a PIPE (what every CI runner gives you) is asynchronous, so a CLI doing it prints nothing at all under CI and works perfectly when tried in a terminal. `check:cli-exit` reads stdout through a pipe for exactly that reason. Commands: `run`, `data-dir` (the first question of every CI failure that turns out to be a misconfigured runner, answerable without running a test), `help`, `--version`.
